What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what finally got me to make the appointment is that I just can’t seem to stop moving around and doing things. For the past year or so, I feel like I’m always puttering—straightening something, fixing a little thing here or there, arranging a drawer, signing up to help with one more activity or errand—and I know I’m 87, so you’d think I’d be better at sitting quietly by now, but I’m not.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be busy all the time exactly, it’s more like if I sit down, part of me is already thinking, “Oh, I should get up and do that other thing.” And then I’m up again. I just have a hard time being still, even when there’s no real reason I need to be doing anything. That’s what’s been bothering me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're sitting still and not doing anything?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I don’t know that I’d call it worry exactly. It’s more like this restless, ants-in-my-chair feeling. Like at the senior center, when we’re sitting through a meeting and they’re going over announcements, I’ll be shifting around in my seat, crossing and uncrossing my legs, fussing with the papers in front of me. And if it goes on too long, I’ll find some excuse to get up—go get coffee, throw something away, straighten the stack of handouts—anything so I’m not just sitting there doing nothing.\n\nChurch is the same way sometimes, especially during the announcements after the service. Everyone else seems able to just sit and listen politely, and I’m sitting there thinking, oh, I need to move, just a little. I don’t feel panicked, exactly. It’s just uncomfortable, like my body won’t settle unless I give it something to do.\n\nDoctor: Has this restlessness been causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I suppose I didn’t want to admit that at first. I kept telling myself, “Oh, I’m just being helpful,” or “I’m just keeping things moving,” but at the senior center it’s started to affect people around me.\n\nAfter a couple of meetings, I actually had to apologize because I realized I’d been up and down, interrupting the flow, fussing with papers and the sign-up sheets, and people were getting a little tense with me. One friend — she was kind about it, but still — suggested maybe I should take a smaller role at the sign-up table for a while, because my manner was making things feel more hectic than they needed to be.\n\nThat hurt my feelings a bit, honestly, because I do want to help. I don’t mean to make anyone uncomfortable. But yes, I’d say it’s caused some problems, at least socially.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I do, more than I like to admit. The clearest place I notice it is in the planning meetings for the exhibit at the senior center. Someone will be explaining an idea, or describing how they think we should arrange something, and before they’ve even finished their sentence I’ll jump in with, “Oh, we could do this,” or “No, what if we put it over there,” or I’ll answer a question that wasn’t quite done being asked.\n\nAnd the awful part is, I usually realize it right after I’ve done it. I can see on their face that I’ve stepped on their words, you know? They were still talking, and I just came barging in with my thought because it was there and I felt like I had to get it out. Then I feel embarrassed and try to pull back, but by then I’ve already interrupted the flow. I don’t mean to be rude. It just comes out before I’ve really stopped to think, “Wait, let them finish.”\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings of restlessness and impulsivity start after something significant happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Well… not after one big thing, exactly. I’ve been like this in little ways for a long time, I suppose, but it’s been especially noticeable for about a year and five months now.\n\nThat’s when the insurance bills and some home repairs started piling up, and I felt pushed into taking on more responsibilities at the senior center. I kept saying yes to things, helping more, being at the table more, organizing more than I probably should have. And once I was in the middle of all that, the restlessness and the blurting things out became much harder to hide. So I wouldn’t say it started from one dramatic event, but it definitely got more obvious around then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed that as the main problem. I mean, at my age there are always little things going on around me — someone walking past, a phone ringing, papers rustling — and of course I notice them. But I don’t know that I’d say I’m easily distracted in the way you mean.\n\nIt’s more that I sometimes have a lot going on in the moment, and I may shift my attention, but I haven’t really thought of myself as someone who can’t pay attention because of things around me. I’d have to pay closer attention to that, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on one thing for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I don’t know that I’ve really noticed myself being unable to focus on one thing for a long time. I mean, sometimes I get tired, or my mind wanders a little, but I tend to think that’s just… normal, especially now.\n\nIf I’m doing something that needs attention, I can usually stay with it, at least as far as I can tell. I haven’t sat down and measured it, you know, so maybe I’m not the best judge. But it hasn’t stood out to me as the main difficulty. I’d have to think about it more, or maybe notice myself over the next few days, because right now nothing clear is coming to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself so intensely focused on an activity that you lost track of time or forgot about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I don’t think so, not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI mean, there are times I get involved in something and an hour goes by, but I think that happens to anyone, doesn’t it? Like if I’m reading something interesting or sorting through papers and I want to finish the pile. But I wouldn’t say I forget the world around me or completely lose track of everything. I’m usually still aware if the phone rings or if someone comes in, or if I need to stop.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a pattern. If it’s happened, it hasn’t been something I’ve thought of as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so focused on something that you lost track of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no — at least not that I can really point to. I know I more or less answered that a moment ago, but when you say “lost track of my surroundings,” I picture being so absorbed that I wouldn’t hear someone come in, or I’d forget where I was, or miss something important happening around me. And I can’t say that’s typical for me.\n\nI may get involved in something, of course. If I’m looking through old papers or trying to finish a task, I might keep at it longer than I intended. But I’m still aware enough of what’s going on around me. If the phone rings or someone speaks to me, I usually notice. So I wouldn’t say I’ve had that experience in any significant way.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and impulsivity at home, when you're alone, or is it mainly in social situations like at the senior center or church?\n\nPatient: It’s at home too, definitely. It’s not just when I’m around people or trying to be helpful at church or the senior center. That’s part of it, yes, because if someone needs a hand I’m very quick to say, “Oh, I can do that,” and then I’ve taken on one more thing before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nBut even when I’m alone in the house, I’m… puttering. That’s the word I keep using. I’ll sit down and then notice a drawer that needs straightening, or a plant that needs trimming, or something on the counter that should be moved, and then that turns into fixing something else, arranging something, checking something. I tell myself I’m just keeping busy, and at my age maybe that sounds like a good thing, but it feels like I have a hard time simply sitting still and letting things be.\n\nIt’s been more noticeable over the past year or so, I’d say. I’m 87, and I know I’m not rushing around like a young person, but inside I still feel like I’m always needing to do the next little thing. So no, it isn’t only social situations. It follows me home, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decline in your memory or cognitive abilities over the past year?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that I have, no. I mean, at 87 I suppose I expect to be a little slower with some things, and I do sometimes walk into a room and think, “Now what did I come in here for?” But I feel like everyone my age says that, and I don’t know that it’s worse than it was before.\n\nOver the past year specifically… I haven’t noticed some big drop-off, if that’s what you mean. I’m still able to follow conversations, keep track of ordinary things, read what I need to read, manage my day-to-day. I may take a little longer to think of a name now and then, but that doesn’t feel new or alarming to me.\n\nSo I’d say no, not a decline that I’ve noticed. But I’m not sure I’d be the best judge of it either — sometimes other people notice those things before you do.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your mood over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s been anything significant, no. I mean, I have ordinary ups and downs, like anyone does. Some days I feel a little more cheerful or more tired of things, and other days I’m perfectly fine. But I wouldn’t say I’ve had a big change in my mood over the past year.\n\nI haven’t noticed myself becoming a different person emotionally, if that’s what you mean. I’m not aware of some major shift, like being persistently low or unusually elated or anything like that. Mostly I feel like myself. Maybe a bit more aware of my age and the limitations that come with it, but that feels normal to me, not like a mood problem.\n\nSo… no, not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with planning, decision-making, or problem-solving over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, planning and decision-making… I suppose it depends what you mean by that. I still feel like I can figure things out around the house and make ordinary decisions. If something needs doing, I can usually think through the steps, maybe a bit slower than I used to, but I don’t feel stuck or confused by it.\n\nSometimes I’ll go back and forth over small things more than I need to — like whether to do one errand before another, or how to organize something — but I’m not sure that’s new this past year. I’ve always had a tendency to fuss over details a little. And I don’t think I’ve had trouble solving problems in the sense of not knowing what to do or needing someone else to step in.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed a real decline there. Nothing that stands out to me as, “Oh, this is different this year.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself so focused on an activity that you couldn't stop thinking about it, even when you weren't doing it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that strongly, no. Like if I’m trying to remember where I put something, or I’m mulling over how to arrange a drawer or a cabinet, it might stay in my mind for a bit.\n\nBut “couldn’t stop thinking about it” sounds more intense than what I experience, I think. I don’t feel taken over by a thought or unable to let it go. At least not that I’ve noticed. Sometimes my mind wanders back to things, but I can usually shift away from it if I need to.\n\nSo I’d say no, not really — unless you mean just being interested in something and thinking about it later, which seems pretty normal to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t think I’ve noticed a significant change, no. I mean, at my age I’m probably not picking up brand-new things as quickly as I might have years ago, but I wouldn’t say there’s been some clear drop over the past year.

If someone shows me something new, I may need them to go through it slowly, or I might want to write it down, but that’s been true for quite a while. I can still learn little things if I take my time with them. It’s not as if I suddenly can’t understand instructions or can’t remember anything new at all.

So… no, nothing that stands out to me as a major change this year. More just ordinary aging, I suppose, if anything.

But yes, I’d say it’s caused some problems, at least socially.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I do, more than I like to admit. The clearest place I notice it is in the planning meetings for the exhibit at the senior center. Someone will be explaining an idea, or describing how they think we should arrange something, and before they’ve even finished their sentence I’ll jump in with, “Oh, we could do this,” or “No, what if we put it over there,” or I’ll answer a question that wasn’t quite done being asked.\n\nAnd the awful part is, I usually realize it right after I’ve done it. I can see on their face that I’ve stepped on their words, you know? They were still talking, and I just came barging in with my thought because it was there and I felt like I had to get it out. Then I feel embarrassed and try to pull back, but by then I’ve already interrupted the flow. I don’t mean to be rude. It just comes out before I’ve really stopped to think, “Wait, let them finish.”\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings of restlessness and impulsivity start after something significant happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Well… not after one big thing, exactly. I’ve been like this in little ways for a long time, I suppose, but it’s been especially noticeable for about a year and five months now.\n\nThat’s when the insurance bills and some home repairs started piling up, and I felt pushed into taking on more responsibilities at the senior center. I kept saying yes to things, helping more, being at the table more, organizing more than I probably should have. And once I was in the middle of all that, the restlessness and the blurting things out became much harder to hide. So I wouldn’t say it started from one dramatic event, but it definitely got more obvious around then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed that as the main problem. I mean, at my age there are always little things going on around me — someone walking past, a phone ringing, papers rustling — and of course I notice them. But I don’t know that I’d say I’m easily distracted in the way you mean.\n\nIt’s more that I sometimes have a lot going on in the moment, and I may shift my attention, but I haven’t really thought of myself as someone who can’t pay attention because of things around me. I’d have to pay closer attention to that, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on one thing for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I don’t know that I’ve really noticed myself being unable to focus on one thing for a long time. I mean, sometimes I get tired, or my mind wanders a little, but I tend to think that’s just… normal, especially now.\n\nIf I’m doing something that needs attention, I can usually stay with it, at least as far as I can tell. I haven’t sat down and measured it, you know, so maybe I’m not the best judge. But it hasn’t stood out to me as the main difficulty. I’d have to think about it more, or maybe notice myself over the next few days, because right now nothing clear is coming to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself so intensely focused on an activity that you lost track of time or forgot about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I don’t think so, not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI mean, there are times I get involved in something and an hour goes by, but I think that happens to anyone, doesn’t it? Like if I’m reading something interesting or sorting through papers and I want to finish the pile. But I wouldn’t say I forget the world around me or completely lose track of everything. I’m usually still aware if the phone rings or if someone comes in, or if I need to stop.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a pattern. If it’s happened, it hasn’t been something I’ve thought of as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so focused on something that you lost track of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no — at least not that I can really point to. I know I more or less answered that a moment ago, but when you say “lost track of my surroundings,” I picture being so absorbed that I wouldn’t hear someone come in, or I’d forget where I was, or miss something important happening around me. And I can’t say that’s typical for me.\n\nI may get involved in something, of course. If I’m looking through old papers or trying to finish a task, I might keep at it longer than I intended. But I’m still aware enough of what’s going on around me. If the phone rings or someone speaks to me, I usually notice. So I wouldn’t say I’ve had that experience in any significant way.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and impulsivity at home, when you're alone, or is it mainly in social situations like at the senior center or church?\n\nPatient: It’s at home too, definitely. It’s not just when I’m around people or trying to be helpful at church or the senior center. That’s part of it, yes, because if someone needs a hand I’m very quick to say, “Oh, I can do that,” and then I’ve taken on one more thing before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nBut even when I’m alone in the house, I’m… puttering. That’s the word I keep using. I’ll sit down and then notice a drawer that needs straightening, or a plant that needs trimming, or something on the counter that should be moved, and then that turns into fixing something else, arranging something, checking something. I tell myself I’m just keeping busy, and at my age maybe that sounds like a good thing, but it feels like I have a hard time simply sitting still and letting things be.\n\nIt’s been more noticeable over the past year or so, I’d say. I’m 87, and I know I’m not rushing around like a young person, but inside I still feel like I’m always needing to do the next little thing. So no, it isn’t only social situations. It follows me home, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decline in your memory or cognitive abilities over the past year?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that I have, no. I mean, at 87 I suppose I expect to be a little slower with some things, and I do sometimes walk into a room and think, “Now what did I come in here for?” But I feel like everyone my age says that, and I don’t know that it’s worse than it was before.\n\nOver the past year specifically… I haven’t noticed some big drop-off, if that’s what you mean. I’m still able to follow conversations, keep track of ordinary things, read what I need to read, manage my day-to-day. I may take a little longer to think of a name now and then, but that doesn’t feel new or alarming to me.\n\nSo I’d say no, not a decline that I’ve noticed. But I’m not sure I’d be the best judge of it either — sometimes other people notice those things before you do.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your mood over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s been anything significant, no. I mean, I have ordinary ups and downs, like anyone does. Some days I feel a little more cheerful or more tired of things, and other days I’m perfectly fine. But I wouldn’t say I’ve had a big change in my mood over the past year.\n\nI haven’t noticed myself becoming a different person emotionally, if that’s what you mean. I’m not aware of some major shift, like being persistently low or unusually elated or anything like that. Mostly I feel like myself. Maybe a bit more aware of my age and the limitations that come with it, but that feels normal to me, not like a mood problem.\n\nSo… no, not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with planning, decision-making, or problem-solving over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, planning and decision-making… I suppose it depends what you mean by that. I still feel like I can figure things out around the house and make ordinary decisions. If something needs doing, I can usually think through the steps, maybe a bit slower than I used to, but I don’t feel stuck or confused by it.\n\nSometimes I’ll go back and forth over small things more than I need to — like whether to do one errand before another, or how to organize something — but I’m not sure that’s new this past year. I’ve always had a tendency to fuss over details a little. And I don’t think I’ve had trouble solving problems in the sense of not knowing what to do or needing someone else to step in.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed a real decline there. Nothing that stands out to me as, “Oh, this is different this year.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself so focused on an activity that you couldn't stop thinking about it, even when you weren't doing it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that strongly, no. I mean, I do get interested in things — a book, or something on television, or some little project around the house — and I may keep thinking about it afterward in an ordinary way. Like if I’m trying to remember where I put something, or I’m mulling over how to arrange a drawer or a cabinet, it might stay in my mind for a bit.\n\nBut “couldn’t stop thinking about it” sounds more intense than what I experience, I think. I don’t feel taken over by a thought or unable to let it go. At least not that I’ve noticed. Sometimes my mind wanders back to things, but I can usually shift away from it if I need to.\n\nSo I’d say no, not really — unless you mean just being interested in something and thinking about it later, which seems pretty normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to learn new things over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed a significant change, no. I mean, at my age I’m probably not picking up brand-new things as quickly as I might have years ago, but I wouldn’t say there’s been some clear drop over the past year.\n\nIf someone shows me something new, I may need them to go through it slowly, or I might want to write it down, but that’s been true for quite a while. I can still learn little things if I take my time with them. It’s not as if I suddenly can’t understand instructions or can’t remember anything new at all.\n\nSo… no, nothing that stands out to me as a major change this year. More just ordinary aging, I suppose, if anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Sometimes my mind wanders back to things, but I can usually shift away from it if I need to.\n\nSo I’d say no, not really — unless you mean just being interested in something and thinking about it later, which seems pretty normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to learn new things over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed a significant change, no. I mean, at my age I’m probably not picking up brand-new things as quickly as I might have years ago, but I wouldn’t say there’s been some clear drop over the past year.\n\nIf someone shows me something new, I may need them to go through it slowly, or I might want to write it down, but that’s been true for quite a while. I can still learn little things if I take my time with them. It’s not as if I suddenly can’t understand instructions or can’t remember anything new at all.\n\nSo… no, nothing that stands out to me as a major change this year. More just ordinary aging, I suppose, if anything.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced any significant changes in your physical health over the past year?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

I mean, planning and decision-making… I suppose it depends what you mean by that. I still feel like I can figure things out around the house and make ordinary decisions. If something needs doing, I can usually think through the steps, maybe a bit slower than I used to, but I don’t feel stuck or confused by it.\n\nSometimes I’ll go back and forth over small things more than I need to — like whether to do one errand before another, or how to organize something — but I’m not sure that’s new this past year. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think so. I mean, nothing significant that I would point to and say, “That changed this year.” At 87, I suppose there are always little things you notice about your body — you know, you’re not quite as quick, you might feel a bit more careful about how you move, that sort of thing — but I wouldn’t call that a major change in my physical health.

I haven’t had anything that stands out to me as a big shift over the past year. If you mean something like a sudden decline or a new problem that changed what I can do day to day, then no, I don’t think I’ve experienced that. Just the usual aging, as far as I can tell.

---------- turn 16 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.9, G31.84\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what finally got me to make the appointment is that I just can’t seem to stop moving around and doing things. For the past year or so, I feel like I’m always puttering—straightening something, fixing a little thing here or there, arranging a drawer, signing up to help with one more activity or errand—and I know I’m 87, so you’d think I’d be better at sitting quietly by now, but I’m not.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be busy all the time exactly, it’s more like if I sit down, part of me is already thinking, “Oh, I should get up and do that other thing.” And then I’m up again. I just have a hard time being still, even when there’s no real reason I need to be doing anything. That’s what’s been bothering me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're sitting still and not doing anything?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I don’t know that I’d call it worry exactly. It’s more like this restless, ants-in-my-chair feeling. Like at the senior center, when we’re sitting through a meeting and they’re going over announcements, I’ll be shifting around in my seat, crossing and uncrossing my legs, fussing with the papers in front of me. And if it goes on too long, I’ll find some excuse to get up—go get coffee, throw something away, straighten the stack of handouts—anything so I’m not just sitting there doing nothing.\n\nChurch is the same way sometimes, especially during the announcements after the service. Everyone else seems able to just sit and listen politely, and I’m sitting there thinking, oh, I need to move, just a little. I don’t feel panicked, exactly. It’s just uncomfortable, like my body won’t settle unless I give it something to do.\n\nDoctor: Has this restlessness been causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I suppose I didn’t want to admit that at first. I kept telling myself, “Oh, I’m just being helpful,” or “I’m just keeping things moving,” but at the senior center it’s started to affect people around me.\n\nAfter a couple of meetings, I actually had to apologize because I realized I’d been up and down, interrupting the flow, fussing with papers and the sign-up sheets, and people were getting a little tense with me. One friend — she was kind about it, but still — suggested maybe I should take a smaller role at the sign-up table for a while, because my manner was making things feel more hectic than they needed to be.\n\nThat hurt my feelings a bit, honestly, because I do want to help. I don’t mean to make anyone uncomfortable. But yes, I’d say it’s caused some problems, at least socially.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I do, more than I like to admit. The clearest place I notice it is in the planning meetings for the exhibit at the senior center. Someone will be explaining an idea, or describing how they think we should arrange something, and before they’ve even finished their sentence I’ll jump in with, “Oh, we could do this,” or “No, what if we put it over there,” or I’ll answer a question that wasn’t quite done being asked.\n\nAnd the awful part is, I usually realize it right after I’ve done it. I can see on their face that I’ve stepped on their words, you know? They were still talking, and I just came barging in with my thought because it was there and I felt like I had to get it out. Then I feel embarrassed and try to pull back, but by then I’ve already interrupted the flow. I don’t mean to be rude. It just comes out before I’ve really stopped to think, “Wait, let them finish.”\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings of restlessness and impulsivity start after something significant happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Well… not after one big thing, exactly. I’ve been like this in little ways for a long time, I suppose, but it’s been especially noticeable for about a year and five months now.\n\nThat’s when the insurance bills and some home repairs started piling up, and I felt pushed into taking on more responsibilities at the senior center. I kept saying yes to things, helping more, being at the table more, organizing more than I probably should have. And once I was in the middle of all that, the restlessness and the blurting things out became much harder to hide. So I wouldn’t say it started from one dramatic event, but it definitely got more obvious around then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed that as the main problem. I mean, at my age there are always little things going on around me — someone walking past, a phone ringing, papers rustling — and of course I notice them. But I don’t know that I’d say I’m easily distracted in the way you mean.\n\nIt’s more that I sometimes have a lot going on in the moment, and I may shift my attention, but I haven’t really thought of myself as someone who can’t pay attention because of things around me. I’d have to pay closer attention to that, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on one thing for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I don’t know that I’ve really noticed myself being unable to focus on one thing for a long time. I mean, sometimes I get tired, or my mind wanders a little, but I tend to think that’s just… normal, especially now.\n\nIf I’m doing something that needs attention, I can usually stay with it, at least as far as I can tell. I haven’t sat down and measured it, you know, so maybe I’m not the best judge. But it hasn’t stood out to me as the main difficulty. I’d have to think about it more, or maybe notice myself over the next few days, because right now nothing clear is coming to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself so intensely focused on an activity that you lost track of time or forgot about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I don’t think so, not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI mean, there are times I get involved in something and an hour goes by, but I think that happens to anyone, doesn’t it? Like if I’m reading something interesting or sorting through papers and I want to finish the pile. But I wouldn’t say I forget the world around me or completely lose track of everything. I’m usually still aware if the phone rings or if someone comes in, or if I need to stop.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a pattern. If it’s happened, it hasn’t been something I’ve thought of as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so focused on something that you lost track of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no — at least not that I can really point to. I know I more or less answered that a moment ago, but when you say “lost track of my surroundings,” I picture being so absorbed that I wouldn’t hear someone come in, or I’d forget where I was, or miss something important happening around me. And I can’t say that’s typical for me.\n\nI may get involved in something, of course. If I’m looking through old papers or trying to finish a task, I might keep at it longer than I intended. But I’m still aware enough of what’s going on around me. If the phone rings or someone speaks to me, I usually notice. So I wouldn’t say I’ve had that experience in any significant way.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and impulsivity at home, when you're alone, or is it mainly in social situations like at the senior center or church?\n\nPatient: It’s at home too, definitely. It’s not just when I’m around people or trying to be helpful at church or the senior center. That’s part of it, yes, because if someone needs a hand I’m very quick to say, “Oh, I can do that,” and then I’ve taken on one more thing before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nBut even when I’m alone in the house, I’m… puttering. That’s the word I keep using. I’ll sit down and then notice a drawer that needs straightening, or a plant that needs trimming, or something on the counter that should be moved, and then that turns into fixing something else, arranging something, checking something. I tell myself I’m just keeping busy, and at my age maybe that sounds like a good thing, but it feels like I have a hard time simply sitting still and letting things be.\n\nIt’s been more noticeable over the past year or so, I’d say. I’m 87, and I know I’m not rushing around like a young person, but inside I still feel like I’m always needing to do the next little thing. So no, it isn’t only social situations. It follows me home, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decline in your memory or cognitive abilities over the past year?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that I have, no. I mean, at 87 I suppose I expect to be a little slower with some things, and I do sometimes walk into a room and think, “Now what did I come in here for?” But I feel like everyone my age says that, and I don’t know that it’s worse than it was before.\n\nOver the past year specifically… I haven’t noticed some big drop-off, if that’s what you mean. I’m still able to follow conversations, keep track of ordinary things, read what I need to read, manage my day-to-day. I may take a little longer to think of a name now and then, but that doesn’t feel new or alarming to me.\n\nSo I’d say no, not a decline that I’ve noticed. But I’m not sure I’d be the best judge of it either — sometimes other people notice those things before you do.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your mood over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s been anything significant, no. I mean, I have ordinary ups and downs, like anyone does. Some days I feel a little more cheerful or more tired of things, and other days I’m perfectly fine. But I wouldn’t say I’ve had a big change in my mood over the past year.\n\nI haven’t noticed myself becoming a different person emotionally, if that’s what you mean. I’m not aware of some major shift, like being persistently low or unusually elated or anything like that. Mostly I feel like myself. Maybe a bit more aware of my age and the limitations that come with it, but that feels normal to me, not like a mood problem.\n\nSo… no, not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with planning, decision-making, or problem-solving over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, planning and decision-making… I suppose it depends what you mean by that. I still feel like I can figure things out around the house and make ordinary decisions. If something needs doing, I can usually think through the steps, maybe a bit slower than I used to, but I don’t feel stuck or confused by it.\n\nSometimes I’ll go back and forth over small things more than I need to — like whether to do one errand before another, or how to organize something — but I’m not sure that’s new this past year. I’ve always had a tendency to fuss over details a little. And I don’t think I’ve had trouble solving problems in the sense of not knowing what to do or needing someone else to step in.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed a real decline there. Nothing that stands out to me as, “Oh, this is different this year.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself so focused on an activity that you couldn't stop thinking about it, even when you weren't doing it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that strongly, no. I mean, I do get interested in things — a book, or something on television, or some little project around the house — and I may keep thinking about it afterward in an ordinary way. Like if I’m trying to remember where I put something, or I’m mulling over how to arrange a drawer or a cabinet, it might stay in my mind for a bit.\n\nBut “couldn’t stop thinking about it” sounds more intense than what I experience, I think. I don’t feel taken over by a thought or unable to let it go. At least not that I’ve noticed. Sometimes my mind wanders back to things, but I can usually shift away from it if I need to.\n\nSo I’d say no, not really — unless you mean just being interested in something and thinking about it later, which seems pretty normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to learn new things over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed a significant change, no. I mean, at my age I’m probably not picking up brand-new things as quickly as I might have years ago, but I wouldn’t say there’s been some clear drop over the past year.\n\nIf someone shows me something new, I may need them to go through it slowly, or I might want to write it down, but that’s been true for quite a while. I don’t think so, not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI mean, there are times I get involved in something and an hour goes by, but I think that happens to anyone, doesn’t it? Like if I’m reading something interesting or sorting through papers and I want to finish the pile. But I wouldn’t say I forget the world around me or completely lose track of everything. I’m usually still aware if the phone rings or if someone comes in, or if I need to stop.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a pattern. If it’s happened, it hasn’t been something I’ve thought of as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so focused on something that you lost track of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no — at least not that I can really point to. I know I more or less answered that a moment ago, but when you say “lost track of my surroundings,” I picture being so absorbed that I wouldn’t hear someone come in, or I’d forget where I was, or miss something important happening around me. And I can’t say that’s typical for me.\n\nI may get involved in something, of course. If I’m looking through old papers or trying to finish a task, I might keep at it longer than I intended. But I’m still aware enough of what’s going on around me. If the phone rings or someone speaks to me, I usually notice. So I wouldn’t say I’ve had that experience in any significant way.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and impulsivity at home, when you're alone, or is it mainly in social situations like at the senior center or church?\n\nPatient: It’s at home too, definitely. It’s not just when I’m around people or trying to be helpful at church or the senior center. That’s part of it, yes, because if someone needs a hand I’m very quick to say, “Oh, I can do that,” and then I’ve taken on one more thing before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nBut even when I’m alone in the house, I’m… puttering. That’s the word I keep using. I’ll sit down and then notice a drawer that needs straightening, or a plant that needs trimming, or something on the counter that should be moved, and then that turns into fixing something else, arranging something, checking something. I tell myself I’m just keeping busy, and at my age maybe that sounds like a good thing, but it feels like I have a hard time simply sitting still and letting things be.\n\nIt’s been more noticeable over the past year or so, I’d say. I’m 87, and I know I’m not rushing around like a young person, but inside I still feel like I’m always needing to do the next little thing. So no, it isn’t only social situations. It follows me home, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decline in your memory or cognitive abilities over the past year?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that I have, no. I mean, at 87 I suppose I expect to be a little slower with some things, and I do sometimes walk into a room and think, “Now what did I come in here for?” But I feel like everyone my age says that, and I don’t know that it’s worse than it was before.\n\nOver the past year specifically… I haven’t noticed some big drop-off, if that’s what you mean. I’m still able to follow conversations, keep track of ordinary things, read what I need to read, manage my day-to-day. I may take a little longer to think of a name now and then, but that doesn’t feel new or alarming to me.\n\nSo I’d say no, not a decline that I’ve noticed. But I’m not sure I’d be the best judge of it either — sometimes other people notice those things before you do.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your mood over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s been anything significant, no. I mean, I have ordinary ups and downs, like anyone does. Some days I feel a little more cheerful or more tired of things, and other days I’m perfectly fine. But I wouldn’t say I’ve had a big change in my mood over the past year.\n\nI haven’t noticed myself becoming a different person emotionally, if that’s what you mean. I’m not aware of some major shift, like being persistently low or unusually elated or anything like that. Mostly I feel like myself. Maybe a bit more aware of my age and the limitations that come with it, but that feels normal to me, not like a mood problem.\n\nSo… no, not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with planning, decision-making, or problem-solving over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, planning and decision-making… I suppose it depends what you mean by that. I still feel like I can figure things out around the house and make ordinary decisions. If something needs doing, I can usually think through the steps, maybe a bit slower than I used to, but I don’t feel stuck or confused by it.\n\nSometimes I’ll go back and forth over small things more than I need to — like whether to do one errand before another, or how to organize something — but I’m not sure that’s new this past year. I’ve always had a tendency to fuss over details a little. And I don’t think I’ve had trouble solving problems in the sense of not knowing what to do or needing someone else to step in.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed a real decline there. Nothing that stands out to me as, “Oh, this is different this year.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself so focused on an activity that you couldn't stop thinking about it, even when you weren't doing it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that strongly, no. Like if I’m trying to remember where I put something, or I’m mulling over how to arrange a drawer or a cabinet, it might stay in my mind for a bit.\n\nBut “couldn’t stop thinking about it” sounds more intense than what I experience, I think. Sometimes my mind wanders back to things, but I can usually shift away from it if I need to.\n\nSo I’d say no, not really — unless you mean just being interested in something and thinking about it later, which seems pretty normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to learn new things over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed a significant change, no. I mean, at my age I’m probably not picking up brand-new things as quickly as I might have years ago, but I wouldn’t say there’s been some clear drop over the past year.\n\nIf someone shows me something new, I may need them to go through it slowly, or I might want to write it down, but that’s been true for quite a while. I can still learn little things if I take my time with them. It’s not as if I suddenly can’t understand instructions or can’t remember anything new at all.\n\nSo… no, nothing that stands out to me as a major change this year. More just ordinary aging, I suppose, if anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your physical health over the past year?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, nothing significant that I would point to and say, “That changed this year.” At 87, I suppose there are always little things you notice about your body — you know, you’re not quite as quick, you might feel a bit more careful about how you move, that sort of thing — but I wouldn’t call that a major change in my physical health.\n\nI haven’t had anything that stands out to me as a big shift over the past year. If you mean something like a sudden decline or a new problem that changed what I can do day to day, then no, I don’t think I’ve experienced that. I don’t know that I’d call it a “state of flow,” exactly. I’ve heard that expression, but it sounds a little grand for what I experience. I can get involved in something, certainly — like reading, or watching a program, or doing a small task around the house — and I might not notice the time passing right away. But I don’t think everything else fades away completely.

Usually I’m still aware of what’s going on around me. If someone speaks to me, or if the phone rings, or if I need to stop and do something else, I can come out of it. I might be mildly absorbed, I suppose, but not in a way that feels unusual or like I’ve lost track of the world.

So I’d say… not really. At least not in the strong sense you’re describing.

I’m usually still aware if the phone rings or if someone comes in, or if I need to stop.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a pattern. So I wouldn’t say I’ve had that experience in any significant way.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and impulsivity at home, when you're alone, or is it mainly in social situations like at the senior center or church?\n\nPatient: It’s at home too, definitely. It’s not just when I’m around people or trying to be helpful at church or the senior center. That’s part of it, yes, because if someone needs a hand I’m very quick to say, “Oh, I can do that,” and then I’ve taken on one more thing before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nBut even when I’m alone in the house, I’m… puttering. That’s the word I keep using. I’ll sit down and then notice a drawer that needs straightening, or a plant that needs trimming, or something on the counter that should be moved, and then that turns into fixing something else, arranging something, checking something. I tell myself I’m just keeping busy, and at my age maybe that sounds like a good thing, but it feels like I have a hard time simply sitting still and letting things be.\n\nIt’s been more noticeable over the past year or so, I’d say. I’m 87, and I know I’m not rushing around like a young person, but inside I still feel like I’m always needing to do the next little thing. So no, it isn’t only social situations. It follows me home, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decline in your memory or cognitive abilities over the past year?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that I have, no. I mean, at 87 I suppose I expect to be a little slower with some things, and I do sometimes walk into a room and think, “Now what did I come in here for?” But I feel like everyone my age says that, and I don’t know that it’s worse than it was before.\n\nOver the past year specifically… I haven’t noticed some big drop-off, if that’s what you mean. I’m still able to follow conversations, keep track of ordinary things, read what I need to read, manage my day-to-day. I may take a little longer to think of a name now and then, but that doesn’t feel new or alarming to me.\n\nSo I’d say no, not a decline that I’ve noticed. But I’m not sure I’d be the best judge of it either — sometimes other people notice those things before you do.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your mood over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s been anything significant, no. I mean, I have ordinary ups and downs, like anyone does. Some days I feel a little more cheerful or more tired of things, and other days I’m perfectly fine. But I wouldn’t say I’ve had a big change in my mood over the past year.\n\nI haven’t noticed myself becoming a different person emotionally, if that’s what you mean. I’m not aware of some major shift, like being persistently low or unusually elated or anything like that. Mostly I feel like myself. Maybe a bit more aware of my age and the limitations that come with it, but that feels normal to me, not like a mood problem.\n\nSo… no, not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with planning, decision-making, or problem-solving over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, planning and decision-making… I suppose it depends what you mean by that. I still feel like I can figure things out around the house and make ordinary decisions. If something needs doing, I can usually think through the steps, maybe a bit slower than I used to, but I don’t feel stuck or confused by it.\n\nSometimes I’ll go back and forth over small things more than I need to — like whether to do one errand before another, or how to organize something — but I’m not sure that’s new this past year. I’ve always had a tendency to fuss over details a little. And I don’t think I’ve had trouble solving problems in the sense of not knowing what to do or needing someone else to step in.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed a real decline there. Nothing that stands out to me as, “Oh, this is different this year.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself so focused on an activity that you couldn't stop thinking about it, even when you weren't doing it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that strongly, no. I mean, I do get interested in things — a book, or something on television, or some little project around the house — and I may keep thinking about it afterward in an ordinary way. Like if I’m trying to remember where I put something, or I’m mulling over how to arrange a drawer or a cabinet, it might stay in my mind for a bit.\n\nBut “couldn’t stop thinking about it” sounds more intense than what I experience, I think. I don’t feel taken over by a thought or unable to let it go. At least not that I’ve noticed. Sometimes my mind wanders back to things, but I can usually shift away from it if I need to.\n\nSo I’d say no, not really — unless you mean just being interested in something and thinking about it later, which seems pretty normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to learn new things over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed a significant change, no. I mean, at my age I’m probably not picking up brand-new things as quickly as I might have years ago, but I wouldn’t say there’s been some clear drop over the past year.\n\nIf someone shows me something new, I may need them to go through it slowly, or I might want to write it down, but that’s been true for quite a while. I can still learn little things if I take my time with them. It’s not as if I suddenly can’t understand instructions or can’t remember anything new at all.\n\nSo… no, nothing that stands out to me as a major change this year. More just ordinary aging, I suppose, if anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your physical health over the past year?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, nothing significant that I would point to and say, “That changed this year.” At 87, I suppose there are always little things you notice about your body — you know, you’re not quite as quick, you might feel a bit more careful about how you move, that sort of thing — but I wouldn’t call that a major change in my physical health.\n\nI haven’t had anything that stands out to me as a big shift over the past year. If you mean something like a sudden decline or a new problem that changed what I can do day to day, then no, I don’t think I’ve experienced that. Just the usual aging, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so absorbed in an activity that you felt like you were in a state of flow, where everything else around you faded into the background?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a “state of flow,” exactly. I’ve heard that expression, but it sounds a little grand for what I experience. I can get involved in something, certainly — like reading, or watching a program, or doing a small task around the house — and I might not notice the time passing right away. But I don’t think everything else fades away completely.\n\nUsually I’m still aware of what’s going on around me. If someone speaks to me, or if the phone rings, or if I need to stop and do something else, I can come out of it. I might be mildly absorbed, I suppose, but not in a way that feels unusual or like I’ve lost track of the world.\n\nSo I’d say… not really. At least not in the strong sense you’re describing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had the ordinary moments where you walk into a room and think, “Now what did I come in here for?” but I wouldn’t call that confusion or disorientation, really. That feels like something most people have from time to time.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F90.9, G31.84\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what finally got me to make the appointment is that I just can’t seem to stop moving around and doing things. For the past year or so, I feel like I’m always puttering—straightening something, fixing a little thing here or there, arranging a drawer, signing up to help with one more activity or errand—and I know I’m 87, so you’d think I’d be better at sitting quietly by now, but I’m not.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to be busy all the time exactly, it’s more like if I sit down, part of me is already thinking, “Oh, I should get up and do that other thing.” And then I’m up again. I just have a hard time being still, even when there’s no real reason I need to be doing anything. That’s what’s been bothering me enough to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're sitting still and not doing anything?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I don’t know that I’d call it worry exactly. It’s more like this restless, ants-in-my-chair feeling. Like at the senior center, when we’re sitting through a meeting and they’re going over announcements, I’ll be shifting around in my seat, crossing and uncrossing my legs, fussing with the papers in front of me. And if it goes on too long, I’ll find some excuse to get up—go get coffee, throw something away, straighten the stack of handouts—anything so I’m not just sitting there doing nothing.\n\nChurch is the same way sometimes, especially during the announcements after the service. Everyone else seems able to just sit and listen politely, and I’m sitting there thinking, oh, I need to move, just a little. I don’t feel panicked, exactly. It’s just uncomfortable, like my body won’t settle unless I give it something to do.\n\nDoctor: Has this restlessness been causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I suppose I didn’t want to admit that at first. I kept telling myself, “Oh, I’m just being helpful,” or “I’m just keeping things moving,” but at the senior center it’s started to affect people around me.\n\nAfter a couple of meetings, I actually had to apologize because I realized I’d been up and down, interrupting the flow, fussing with papers and the sign-up sheets, and people were getting a little tense with me. One friend — she was kind about it, but still — suggested maybe I should take a smaller role at the sign-up table for a while, because my manner was making things feel more hectic than they needed to be.\n\nThat hurt my feelings a bit, honestly, because I do want to help. I don’t mean to make anyone uncomfortable. But yes, I’d say it’s caused some problems, at least socially.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think I do, more than I like to admit. The clearest place I notice it is in the planning meetings for the exhibit at the senior center. Someone will be explaining an idea, or describing how they think we should arrange something, and before they’ve even finished their sentence I’ll jump in with, “Oh, we could do this,” or “No, what if we put it over there,” or I’ll answer a question that wasn’t quite done being asked.\n\nAnd the awful part is, I usually realize it right after I’ve done it. I can see on their face that I’ve stepped on their words, you know? They were still talking, and I just came barging in with my thought because it was there and I felt like I had to get it out. Then I feel embarrassed and try to pull back, but by then I’ve already interrupted the flow. I don’t mean to be rude. It just comes out before I’ve really stopped to think, “Wait, let them finish.”\n\nDoctor: Did these feelings of restlessness and impulsivity start after something significant happened in your life?\n\nPatient: Well… not after one big thing, exactly. I’ve been like this in little ways for a long time, I suppose, but it’s been especially noticeable for about a year and five months now.\n\nThat’s when the insurance bills and some home repairs started piling up, and I felt pushed into taking on more responsibilities at the senior center. I kept saying yes to things, helping more, being at the table more, organizing more than I probably should have. And once I was in the middle of all that, the restlessness and the blurting things out became much harder to hide. So I wouldn’t say it started from one dramatic event, but it definitely got more obvious around then.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed that as the main problem. I mean, at my age there are always little things going on around me — someone walking past, a phone ringing, papers rustling — and of course I notice them. But I don’t know that I’d say I’m easily distracted in the way you mean.\n\nIt’s more that I sometimes have a lot going on in the moment, and I may shift my attention, but I haven’t really thought of myself as someone who can’t pay attention because of things around me. I’d have to pay closer attention to that, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on one thing for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way, exactly. I don’t know that I’ve really noticed myself being unable to focus on one thing for a long time. I mean, sometimes I get tired, or my mind wanders a little, but I tend to think that’s just… normal, especially now.\n\nIf I’m doing something that needs attention, I can usually stay with it, at least as far as I can tell. I haven’t sat down and measured it, you know, so maybe I’m not the best judge. But it hasn’t stood out to me as the main difficulty. I’d have to think about it more, or maybe notice myself over the next few days, because right now nothing clear is coming to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself so intensely focused on an activity that you lost track of time or forgot about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I don’t think so, not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI mean, there are times I get involved in something and an hour goes by, but I think that happens to anyone, doesn’t it? Like if I’m reading something interesting or sorting through papers and I want to finish the pile. But I wouldn’t say I forget the world around me or completely lose track of everything. I’m usually still aware if the phone rings or if someone comes in, or if I need to stop.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a pattern. If it’s happened, it hasn’t been something I’ve thought of as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so focused on something that you lost track of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no — at least not that I can really point to. I know I more or less answered that a moment ago, but when you say “lost track of my surroundings,” I picture being so absorbed that I wouldn’t hear someone come in, or I’d forget where I was, or miss something important happening around me. And I can’t say that’s typical for me.\n\nI may get involved in something, of course. If I’m looking through old papers or trying to finish a task, I might keep at it longer than I intended. But I’m still aware enough of what’s going on around me. If the phone rings or someone speaks to me, I usually notice. So I wouldn’t say I’ve had that experience in any significant way.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and impulsivity at home, when you're alone, or is it mainly in social situations like at the senior center or church?\n\nPatient: It’s at home too, definitely. It’s not just when I’m around people or trying to be helpful at church or the senior center. That’s part of it, yes, because if someone needs a hand I’m very quick to say, “Oh, I can do that,” and then I’ve taken on one more thing before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nBut even when I’m alone in the house, I’m… puttering. That’s the word I keep using. I’ll sit down and then notice a drawer that needs straightening, or a plant that needs trimming, or something on the counter that should be moved, and then that turns into fixing something else, arranging something, checking something. I tell myself I’m just keeping busy, and at my age maybe that sounds like a good thing, but it feels like I have a hard time simply sitting still and letting things be.\n\nIt’s been more noticeable over the past year or so, I’d say. I’m 87, and I know I’m not rushing around like a young person, but inside I still feel like I’m always needing to do the next little thing. So no, it isn’t only social situations. It follows me home, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decline in your memory or cognitive abilities over the past year?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that I have, no. I mean, at 87 I suppose I expect to be a little slower with some things, and I do sometimes walk into a room and think, “Now what did I come in here for?” But I feel like everyone my age says that, and I don’t know that it’s worse than it was before.\n\nOver the past year specifically… I haven’t noticed some big drop-off, if that’s what you mean. I’m still able to follow conversations, keep track of ordinary things, read what I need to read, manage my day-to-day. I may take a little longer to think of a name now and then, but that doesn’t feel new or alarming to me.\n\nSo I’d say no, not a decline that I’ve noticed. But I’m not sure I’d be the best judge of it either — sometimes other people notice those things before you do.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your mood over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s been anything significant, no. I mean, I have ordinary ups and downs, like anyone does. Some days I feel a little more cheerful or more tired of things, and other days I’m perfectly fine. But I wouldn’t say I’ve had a big change in my mood over the past year.\n\nI haven’t noticed myself becoming a different person emotionally, if that’s what you mean. I’m not aware of some major shift, like being persistently low or unusually elated or anything like that. Mostly I feel like myself. Maybe a bit more aware of my age and the limitations that come with it, but that feels normal to me, not like a mood problem.\n\nSo… no, not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with planning, decision-making, or problem-solving over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, planning and decision-making… I suppose it depends what you mean by that. I still feel like I can figure things out around the house and make ordinary decisions. If something needs doing, I can usually think through the steps, maybe a bit slower than I used to, but I don’t feel stuck or confused by it.\n\nSometimes I’ll go back and forth over small things more than I need to — like whether to do one errand before another, or how to organize something — but I’m not sure that’s new this past year. I’ve always had a tendency to fuss over details a little. And I don’t think I’ve had trouble solving problems in the sense of not knowing what to do or needing someone else to step in.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed a real decline there. Nothing that stands out to me as, “Oh, this is different this year.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself so focused on an activity that you couldn't stop thinking about it, even when you weren't doing it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that strongly, no. I mean, I do get interested in things — a book, or something on television, or some little project around the house — and I may keep thinking about it afterward in an ordinary way. Like if I’m trying to remember where I put something, or I’m mulling over how to arrange a drawer or a cabinet, it might stay in my mind for a bit.\n\nBut “couldn’t stop thinking about it” sounds more intense than what I experience, I think. I don’t feel taken over by a thought or unable to let it go. At least not that I’ve noticed. Sometimes my mind wanders back to things, but I can usually shift away from it if I need to.\n\nSo I’d say no, not really — unless you mean just being interested in something and thinking about it later, which seems pretty normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to learn new things over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed a significant change, no. I mean, at my age I’m probably not picking up brand-new things as quickly as I might have years ago, but I wouldn’t say there’s been some clear drop over the past year.\n\nIf someone shows me something new, I may need them to go through it slowly, or I might want to write it down, but that’s been true for quite a while. I can still learn little things if I take my time with them. More just ordinary aging, I suppose, if anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your physical health over the past year?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Just the usual aging, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so absorbed in an activity that you felt like you were in a state of flow, where everything else around you faded into the background?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a “state of flow,” exactly. I’d have to think about it more, or maybe notice myself over the next few days, because right now nothing clear is coming to mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself so intensely focused on an activity that you lost track of time or forgot about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I don’t think so, not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI mean, there are times I get involved in something and an hour goes by, but I think that happens to anyone, doesn’t it? Like if I’m reading something interesting or sorting through papers and I want to finish the pile. But I wouldn’t say I forget the world around me or completely lose track of everything. I’m usually still aware if the phone rings or if someone comes in, or if I need to stop.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t really noticed that as a pattern. If it’s happened, it hasn’t been something I’ve thought of as a problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so focused on something that you lost track of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no — at least not that I can really point to. I know I more or less answered that a moment ago, but when you say “lost track of my surroundings,” I picture being so absorbed that I wouldn’t hear someone come in, or I’d forget where I was, or miss something important happening around me. And I can’t say that’s typical for me.\n\nI may get involved in something, of course. If I’m looking through old papers or trying to finish a task, I might keep at it longer than I intended. But I’m still aware enough of what’s going on around me. If the phone rings or someone speaks to me, I usually notice. So I wouldn’t say I’ve had that experience in any significant way.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this restlessness and impulsivity at home, when you're alone, or is it mainly in social situations like at the senior center or church?\n\nPatient: It’s at home too, definitely. It’s not just when I’m around people or trying to be helpful at church or the senior center. That’s part of it, yes, because if someone needs a hand I’m very quick to say, “Oh, I can do that,” and then I’ve taken on one more thing before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nBut even when I’m alone in the house, I’m… puttering. That’s the word I keep using. I’ll sit down and then notice a drawer that needs straightening, or a plant that needs trimming, or something on the counter that should be moved, and then that turns into fixing something else, arranging something, checking something. I tell myself I’m just keeping busy, and at my age maybe that sounds like a good thing, but it feels like I have a hard time simply sitting still and letting things be.\n\nIt’s been more noticeable over the past year or so, I’d say. I’m 87, and I know I’m not rushing around like a young person, but inside I still feel like I’m always needing to do the next little thing. So no, it isn’t only social situations. It follows me home, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decline in your memory or cognitive abilities over the past year?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that I have, no. I mean, at 87 I suppose I expect to be a little slower with some things, and I do sometimes walk into a room and think, “Now what did I come in here for?” But I feel like everyone my age says that, and I don’t know that it’s worse than it was before.\n\nOver the past year specifically… I haven’t noticed some big drop-off, if that’s what you mean. I’m still able to follow conversations, keep track of ordinary things, read what I need to read, manage my day-to-day. I may take a little longer to think of a name now and then, but that doesn’t feel new or alarming to me.\n\nSo I’d say no, not a decline that I’ve noticed. But I’m not sure I’d be the best judge of it either — sometimes other people notice those things before you do.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your mood over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s been anything significant, no. I mean, I have ordinary ups and downs, like anyone does. Some days I feel a little more cheerful or more tired of things, and other days I’m perfectly fine. But I wouldn’t say I’ve had a big change in my mood over the past year.\n\nI haven’t noticed myself becoming a different person emotionally, if that’s what you mean. I’m not aware of some major shift, like being persistently low or unusually elated or anything like that. Mostly I feel like myself. Maybe a bit more aware of my age and the limitations that come with it, but that feels normal to me, not like a mood problem.\n\nSo… no, not that I can really point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulty with planning, decision-making, or problem-solving over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in any major way. I mean, planning and decision-making… I suppose it depends what you mean by that. I still feel like I can figure things out around the house and make ordinary decisions. If something needs doing, I can usually think through the steps, maybe a bit slower than I used to, but I don’t feel stuck or confused by it.\n\nSometimes I’ll go back and forth over small things more than I need to — like whether to do one errand before another, or how to organize something — but I’m not sure that’s new this past year. I’ve always had a tendency to fuss over details a little. And I don’t think I’ve had trouble solving problems in the sense of not knowing what to do or needing someone else to step in.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed a real decline there. Nothing that stands out to me as, “Oh, this is different this year.”\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself so focused on an activity that you couldn't stop thinking about it, even when you weren't doing it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that strongly, no. I mean, I do get interested in things — a book, or something on television, or some little project around the house — and I may keep thinking about it afterward in an ordinary way. Like if I’m trying to remember where I put something, or I’m mulling over how to arrange a drawer or a cabinet, it might stay in my mind for a bit.\n\nBut “couldn’t stop thinking about it” sounds more intense than what I experience, I think. I don’t feel taken over by a thought or unable to let it go. At least not that I’ve noticed. Sometimes my mind wanders back to things, but I can usually shift away from it if I need to.\n\nSo I’d say no, not really — unless you mean just being interested in something and thinking about it later, which seems pretty normal to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your ability to learn new things over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed a significant change, no. I can still learn little things if I take my time with them. It’s not as if I suddenly can’t understand instructions or can’t remember anything new at all.\n\nSo… no, nothing that stands out to me as a major change this year. Just the usual aging, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become so absorbed in an activity that you felt like you were in a state of flow, where everything else around you faded into the background?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a “state of flow,” exactly.
